# 09. ServingRuntime
**Course:** OpenShift AI Architecture — Corporate L3
**Audience:** Senior OpenShift/Kubernetes platform engineers, MLOps engineers, SREs, security engineers, and architects with approximately 10+ years of infrastructure experience
**Production baseline used:** Red Hat OpenShift AI Self-Managed 3.4.2 GA, checked 2026-07-21
**Preview policy:** OpenShift AI 3.5 Early Access concepts are not treated as production-supported design.
**Scope:** Self-managed OpenShift AI on OpenShift Container Platform; adapt cloud-provider, storage, network, and security details to the supported configuration matrix.
> This is an architecture and build workbook, not a replacement for the Red Hat supported-configuration matrix, release notes, or a vendor support case. Validate every CRD field against the exact installed release by using `oc explain` and server-side dry run.
## 1. Executive summary
ServingRuntime concerns runtime templates that declare supported model formats, serving images, command-line arguments, ports, resource conventions, protocol capabilities, and scheduling requirements.
At Corporate L3 level, the goal is not merely to make the feature work. The platform must be supportable, observable, secure, repeatable, capacity-aware, upgradeable, and recoverable.
The engineer must understand which objects are declarative control-plane inputs, which pods execute user data-plane workloads, which dependencies hold state, and which team owns each failure domain.
A production design should be delivered through version control, peer review, policy checks, server-side validation, staged rollout, measurable service objectives, and a tested rollback or recovery procedure.
### 1.1 Learning outcomes
- Explain ServingRuntime to architects, administrators, data scientists, security teams, and incident responders.
- Map dashboard actions to Kubernetes/OpenShift custom resources and controller reconciliation.
- Build a lab implementation from an empty project using CLI-first, auditable steps.
- Apply RBAC, quotas, network isolation, storage protection, TLS trust, and secret controls.
- Define service-level indicators, dashboards, alerts, and evidence for incident response.
- Troubleshoot failures from user symptom to API object, event, controller log, pod, network path, and dependency.
- Design upgrade, high-availability, and disaster-recovery controls without relying on unsupported assumptions.
- Convert the lab into a GitOps-managed enterprise pattern with validation and change approval.
## 2. Supported-version and lifecycle guardrails
- Use OpenShift AI 3.4.2 GA as the production baseline for this guide.
- Treat OpenShift AI 3.5 Early Access as evaluation-only until Red Hat publishes a supported GA release and supported-configuration matrix.
- Review deprecations before every upgrade. In the 3.4 generation, legacy ModelMesh, KServe Serverless deployment mode, older TrustyAI APIs, older model-registry APIs, and some legacy dashboard configuration paths require migration planning.
- Prefer KServe RawDeployment patterns where supported by the product documentation and organization requirements.
- Do not install unrelated ISV applications into OpenShift AI product namespaces.
- Do not copy upstream manifests blindly into a supported OpenShift AI installation. Product operators own many operands and may reconcile or overwrite them.
- Pin approved images by digest where the support model permits, but never override operator-managed images outside documented procedures.
- Record the OpenShift version, OpenShift AI version, operator channel, CSV, CRD versions, GPU stack, storage stack, and known issues in every architecture decision record.
- Run pre-upgrade validation in a representative non-production cluster with the same topology, identity, storage, proxy, certificate, and accelerator constraints.
- Keep rollback expectations realistic: Kubernetes API migrations, databases, and metadata schemas may not support simple binary rollback.
## 3. Architecture context
OpenShift AI is operator-managed and integrates service-layer capabilities such as the dashboard, workbenches, AI pipelines, model serving, monitoring, and related AI services. The OpenShift AI Operator is the management layer that deploys and maintains components and sub-operators.
Common platform namespaces include `redhat-ods-operator` for the operator and `redhat-ods-applications` for the dashboard and related managed components. User projects hold tenant workloads and project-scoped resources.
### 3.1 Primary resources
- **ServingRuntime:** identify its owner, API group/version, reconciliation controller, namespace scope, status conditions, finalizers, and upgrade behavior.
- **ClusterServingRuntime:** identify its owner, API group/version, reconciliation controller, namespace scope, status conditions, finalizers, and upgrade behavior.
- **InferenceService:** identify its owner, API group/version, reconciliation controller, namespace scope, status conditions, finalizers, and upgrade behavior.
- **Deployment:** identify its owner, API group/version, reconciliation controller, namespace scope, status conditions, finalizers, and upgrade behavior.
- **Container image:** identify its owner, API group/version, reconciliation controller, namespace scope, status conditions, finalizers, and upgrade behavior.
- **ConfigMap:** identify its owner, API group/version, reconciliation controller, namespace scope, status conditions, finalizers, and upgrade behavior.
- **Secret:** identify its owner, API group/version, reconciliation controller, namespace scope, status conditions, finalizers, and upgrade behavior.
### 3.2 Relevant namespaces
- `user project namespaces`
- `redhat-ods-applications`
### 3.3 Dependencies
- KServe
- model server image
- container registry
- accelerator stack
- model format
### 3.4 Logical data flows
1. InferenceService model format -> runtime selection -> pod template.
2. runtime container -> model load -> inference API.
### 3.5 Control-plane versus data-plane interpretation
The control plane accepts desired state, validates it, stores it in the Kubernetes API, and reconciles subordinate objects. The data plane executes notebooks, pipeline tasks, training jobs, model servers, storage transfers, and inference requests.
A healthy API object does not prove a healthy data path. Validate both reconciliation status and an end-to-end transaction using realistic credentials, artifacts, network paths, and resource requests.

| Capability | Responsible | Accountable | Consulted | Informed |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| OpenShift cluster health | OpenShift platform team | SRE | security/network/storage teams | AI product teams |
| ServingRuntime | AI platform team | AI platform architect | data science/MLOps/SRE | service owners |
| Identity and groups | IAM team | security owner | AI platform team | users |
| Network and DNS | network/platform team | network owner | security/SRE | AI users |
| Persistent and object storage | storage team | storage owner | AI platform/MLOps | users |
| Model and dataset governance | model owner/MLOps | business risk owner | security/legal/data governance | platform team |
| Monitoring and incident response | SRE | service owner | platform/MLOps/vendor support | stakeholders |
| Backup and restore | platform/storage/database owners | DR owner | security/application owners | users |
## 5. Discovery before implementation
1. Capture cluster and operator versions.
2. Confirm the exact supported OpenShift/OpenShift AI combination.
3. List installed CRDs and API resources.
4. Identify operator-managed namespaces and operands.
5. Confirm cluster-wide proxy, trusted CA, ingress certificate, and DNS behavior.
6. List storage classes, volume modes, expansion support, snapshot classes, and reclaim policies.
7. Identify S3 endpoints, authentication model, encryption, versioning, lifecycle, backup, and egress rules.
8. Map node roles, zones, taints, labels, CPU architecture, GPU models, MIG/time-slicing policy, and capacity.
9. Verify user-workload monitoring and log collection.
10. Export current RBAC and group membership relevant to OpenShift AI.
11. Check quotas, limit ranges, network policies, admission policies, and image policies.
12. Record known issues from the release notes that match enabled components.
```bash
oc version
oc get clusterversion
oc get csv,subscription,installplan -A
oc get datasciencecluster -o yaml
oc get dscinitialization -o yaml
oc get crd | grep -iE 'opendatahub|datascience|kubeflow|kserve|trusty|modelregistry'
oc api-resources | grep -iE 'notebook|pipeline|inference|serving|trusty|registry'
oc get pods -n redhat-ods-operator -o wide
oc get pods -n redhat-ods-applications -o wide
oc get storageclass,volumesnapshotclass
oc get nodes -L topology.kubernetes.io/zone,node-role.kubernetes.io/worker
```
## 6. Detailed architecture
### 6.1 Operator ownership
Operator ownership: distinguish supported configuration surfaces from generated operands.
### 6.2 Namespace boundaries
Namespace boundaries: keep tenant workloads in user projects and product operands in product namespaces.
### 6.3 API-driven lifecycle
API-driven lifecycle: desired state is represented in CRs; controller conditions and events are primary evidence.
### 6.4 State classification
State classification: identify which state lives in etcd, PVCs, object storage, databases, image registries, Git, or external systems.
### 6.5 Identity propagation
Identity propagation: browser user, OAuth token, service account, object-store credential, registry credential, and model endpoint identity are separate security contexts.
### 6.6 Network path decomposition
Network path decomposition: client, router, service, endpoint, pod, sidecar, upstream storage/database, and external API must each be tested.
### 6.7 Resource isolation
Resource isolation: CPU, memory, ephemeral storage, PVC capacity, GPU, network bandwidth, and API rate limits require explicit governance.
### 6.8 Observability by contract
Observability by contract: every production component needs availability, latency, error, saturation, and dependency indicators.
### 6.9 Failure-domain awareness
Failure-domain awareness: replicas must be placed across nodes/zones and must not share hidden single points of failure.
### 6.10 Change safety
Change safety: configurations must be rendered, validated, diffed, approved, rolled out, observed, and reversible.
## 7. From-scratch implementation project
**Project objective:** deploy and validate a governed lab implementation of **ServingRuntime**, then convert it into a reusable corporate pattern.
### 7.1 Phase 0 — Define success criteria
1. Write the user journey and API transaction that proves the capability works.
2. Define availability, latency, throughput, capacity, security, and recovery acceptance criteria.
3. Name the owning team, escalation path, maintenance window, and support entitlement.
4. Document which features are GA, Technology Preview, Developer Preview, deprecated, or unsupported.
### 7.2 Phase 1 — Prepare the cluster
1. Validate cluster operators and node readiness.
2. Verify time synchronization, DNS, ingress, proxy, trusted CA, image registry, and storage.
3. Confirm OpenShift AI operator and DataScienceCluster reconciliation.
4. Capture a pre-change must-gather or targeted support snapshot for regulated environments.
### 7.3 Phase 2 — Create the project boundary
1. Create the project through GitOps or an approved project-request workflow.
2. Apply ownership labels, annotations, quota, limits, and default-deny network policy.
3. Bind groups rather than individual users whenever possible.
4. Create service accounts with token automount disabled unless an API token is required.
### 7.4 Phase 3 — Configure dependencies
1. Create storage, object-store connections, registry pull credentials, and CA bundles through approved secret delivery.
2. Permit only required DNS, object-store, registry, database, monitoring, and API egress.
3. Validate dependency health independently before creating the AI resource.
4. Record endpoint, certificate chain, authentication method, timeout, retry, and ownership.
### 7.5 Phase 4 — Create the OpenShift AI resource
1. Use the dashboard once in a lab to learn the supported resource shape.
2. Export the resource, strip generated metadata/status, replace credentials, and commit it to Git.
3. Run schema validation and `oc apply --dry-run=server` against the target cluster.
4. Apply through the controlled delivery pipeline and observe controller events and conditions.
### 7.6 Phase 5 — Validate the data path
1. Run a functional transaction using a non-admin identity.
2. Verify storage reads/writes, model or notebook startup, pipeline execution, or inference as applicable.
3. Measure cold start, steady-state latency, throughput, errors, and resource consumption.
4. Test a denied path to prove isolation and least privilege.
### 7.7 Phase 6 — Add production controls
1. Add alert rules, dashboards, log queries, SLO recording rules, and runbook links.
2. Add PDB/topology/anti-affinity only after confirming the operator-supported customization point.
3. Add backup, retention, secret rotation, image promotion, vulnerability scanning, and policy checks.
4. Run node drain, dependency interruption, credential rotation, and rollback tests.
### 7.8 Phase 7 — Handover
1. Publish architecture diagram, dependency map, RACI, SLO, dashboards, alerts, runbooks, and recovery procedure.
2. Train service desk and L2 teams on symptom classification and evidence collection.
3. Define monthly capacity review, quarterly access review, and scheduled recovery exercises.
4. Record technical debt, unsupported exceptions, and migration dates for deprecated features.

## 9. Security architecture
1. Use enterprise identity-provider groups and periodic access recertification.
2. Separate platform administrator, project administrator, editor, operator, observer, and auditor duties.
3. Avoid broad `cluster-admin`, wildcard resources, wildcard verbs, and direct bindings to personal accounts.
4. Disable service-account token automount unless the workload calls the Kubernetes API.
5. Keep object-store, database, registry, Git, and endpoint credentials separate and independently rotatable.
6. Use external secret management or an approved secret-injection process.
7. Encrypt traffic, validate the complete certificate chain, and document private CA distribution.
8. Apply default-deny network policy and open only verified flows.
9. Restrict image registries and require vulnerability scanning, signing/attestation, and immutable promotion.
10. Use SCC/Pod Security controls appropriate to the runtime; do not grant privileged access merely to bypass an error.
11. Log authentication, authorization, resource changes, secret access patterns, and model promotion decisions.
12. Classify datasets, features, prompts, outputs, model artifacts, and telemetry for privacy and retention.
13. Protect model endpoints against unauthorized access, abuse, excessive tokens/requests, and data exfiltration.
14. Test negative cases: unauthorized user, wrong group, expired credential, blocked egress, untrusted CA, and denied namespace access.
15. Document every security exception with owner, risk, compensating control, expiry, and removal plan.
## 10. Capacity and performance engineering
| Dimension | L3 engineering guidance |
|---|---|
| CPU | requests drive scheduling; limits can create throttling; observe throttled seconds and run-queue pressure. |
| Memory | model load, notebook kernels, pipeline tasks, caches, sidecars, and JVM/Python overhead require headroom. |
| GPU | model size, precision, KV cache, batch size, concurrency, MIG profile, and sharing mode determine capacity. |
| Storage capacity | datasets, checkpoints, model artifacts, caches, logs, metadata, and temporary files grow differently. |
| Storage performance | measure throughput, IOPS, latency, metadata operations, and object-store multipart behavior. |
| Network | model downloads, distributed training, artifact movement, inference payloads, and telemetry can saturate links. |
| API/control plane | large numbers of CRs, events, pipeline tasks, pods, and reconciles can stress API and controllers. |
| Cold start | image pull, scheduling, volume attach, model download, compilation, and readiness dominate startup. |
| Concurrency | test representative payloads, arrival patterns, timeouts, retries, and downstream dependencies. |
| Cost | tag ownership, environment, model, GPU class, storage tier, and lifecycle to support showback/chargeback. |

## 11. Observability and SLO design
| SLI | Measurement approach |
|---|---|
| Availability | successful user/API transactions divided by valid attempts |
| Latency | P50/P95/P99 end-to-end transaction duration |
| Errors | failed reconciles, HTTP errors, task failures, model-load failures, and dependency errors |
| Saturation | CPU, memory, GPU, storage, network, queue, API, and controller workqueue pressure |
| Freshness | age of model, metadata, pipeline result, monitoring data, or backup |
| Recovery | time to restore service and maximum data loss during exercises |
- Alert 01: component or endpoint unavailable; include cluster, namespace, resource, owner, severity, first diagnostic command, and runbook URL.
- Alert 02: controller reconcile errors above baseline; include cluster, namespace, resource, owner, severity, first diagnostic command, and runbook URL.
- Alert 03: resource remains non-ready beyond the defined startup objective; include cluster, namespace, resource, owner, severity, first diagnostic command, and runbook URL.
- Alert 04: pod restart or crash-loop rate; include cluster, namespace, resource, owner, severity, first diagnostic command, and runbook URL.
- Alert 05: persistent volume nearly full or object-store failures; include cluster, namespace, resource, owner, severity, first diagnostic command, and runbook URL.
- Alert 06: database connection saturation or replication lag; include cluster, namespace, resource, owner, severity, first diagnostic command, and runbook URL.
- Alert 07: GPU Xid errors, unavailable devices, or DCGM exporter failure; include cluster, namespace, resource, owner, severity, first diagnostic command, and runbook URL.
- Alert 08: certificate expiry and failed TLS handshakes; include cluster, namespace, resource, owner, severity, first diagnostic command, and runbook URL.
- Alert 09: authentication or authorization failure spike; include cluster, namespace, resource, owner, severity, first diagnostic command, and runbook URL.
- Alert 10: pipeline task failure rate; include cluster, namespace, resource, owner, severity, first diagnostic command, and runbook URL.
- Alert 11: model inference error or latency budget breach; include cluster, namespace, resource, owner, severity, first diagnostic command, and runbook URL.
- Alert 12: TrustyAI data/metric freshness breach; include cluster, namespace, resource, owner, severity, first diagnostic command, and runbook URL.
- Alert 13: backup failure or stale recovery point; include cluster, namespace, resource, owner, severity, first diagnostic command, and runbook URL.
- Alert 14: quota saturation or pending pods; include cluster, namespace, resource, owner, severity, first diagnostic command, and runbook URL.
- Alert 15: high cardinality or telemetry ingestion overload; include cluster, namespace, resource, owner, severity, first diagnostic command, and runbook URL.

```yaml
    platform.example.com/topic: servingruntime
    platform.example.com/environment: lab
    platform.example.com/owner: ml-platform
```
### 12.2 ResourceQuota
```yaml
apiVersion: v1
kind: ResourceQuota
metadata:
  name: ai-project-quota
spec:
  hard:
    requests.cpu: "16"
    requests.memory: 64Gi
    limits.cpu: "32"
    limits.memory: 128Gi
    requests.storage: 500Gi
    persistentvolumeclaims: "20"
    pods: "100"
    services: "30"
    secrets: "100"
    configmaps: "100"
```
### 12.3 LimitRange
```yaml
apiVersion: v1
kind: LimitRange
metadata:
  name: ai-default-limits
spec:
  limits:
  - type: Container
    defaultRequest:
      cpu: 250m
      memory: 512Mi
    default:
      cpu: "2"
      memory: 4Gi
```
### 12.4 Default-deny NetworkPolicy
```yaml
apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: NetworkPolicy
metadata:
  name: default-deny-all
spec:
  podSelector: {}
  policyTypes:
  - Ingress
  - Egress
```
### 12.5 Permit DNS egress
```yaml
apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: NetworkPolicy
metadata:
  name: allow-dns
spec:
  podSelector: {}
  policyTypes:
  - Egress
  egress:
  - to:
    - namespaceSelector:
        matchLabels:
          kubernetes.io/metadata.name: openshift-dns
    ports:
    - protocol: UDP
      port: 53
    - protocol: TCP
      port: 53
```
### 12.6 Project service account
```yaml
apiVersion: v1
kind: ServiceAccount
metadata:
  name: ai-workload
automountServiceAccountToken: false
```
### 12.7 Read-only observer Role
```yaml
apiVersion: rbac.authorization.k8s.io/v1
kind: Role
metadata:
  name: ai-observer
  labels:
    opendatahub.io/dashboard: "true"
rules:
- apiGroups: [""]
  resources: ["pods","pods/log","services","events","configmaps","persistentvolumeclaims"]
  verbs: ["get","list","watch"]
- apiGroups: ["kubeflow.org"]
  resources: ["notebooks"]
  verbs: ["get","list","watch"]
- apiGroups: ["serving.kserve.io"]
  resources: ["inferenceservices","servingruntimes"]
  verbs: ["get","list","watch"]
```
## 13. Topic-specific implementation examples
> **Version rule:** before applying any CR, run `oc api-resources`, `oc explain`, and export a dashboard-created sample from the target cluster. CRD versions and supported fields change between OpenShift AI releases.
### 13.1 Illustrative namespace-scoped ServingRuntime
```yaml
apiVersion: serving.kserve.io/v1alpha1
kind: ServingRuntime
metadata:
  name: corporate-onnx-runtime
  labels:
    opendatahub.io/dashboard: "true"
spec:
  supportedModelFormats:
  - name: onnx
    version: "1"
    autoSelect: true
    priority: 10
  protocolVersions:
  - v2
  multiModel: false
  containers:
  - name: kserve-container
    image: registry.example.com/ai/onnx-runtime@sha256:REPLACE_WITH_APPROVED_DIGEST
    ports:
    - containerPort: 8080
      protocol: TCP
    resources:
      requests:
        cpu: "1"
        memory: 2Gi
      limits:
        cpu: "4"
        memory: 8Gi
```
## 14. Hands-on laboratories
### Lab 01 — Inventory and dependency map
**Objective:** Discover live APIs, operands, routes, storage, network policies, and identity dependencies; produce a diagram and ownership table.
**Prerequisites:** non-production cluster access, approved namespace, CLI, Git repository, and permission to create only the scoped resources.
**Procedure:**
1. Open a change or lab record and state the hypothesis.
2. Capture pre-test cluster, component, resource, event, and metric state.
3. Apply the smallest controlled change.
4. Watch API conditions, controller logs, generated pods, events, routes/services, and dependencies.
5. Execute the user-level transaction with a non-admin identity.
6. Collect latency, error, saturation, security, and audit evidence.
7. Revert or complete the change through the same controlled mechanism.
8. Compare post-test state with the baseline.
9. Document findings, defect IDs, risk, owner, and next action.
10. Commit sanitized manifests and runbook updates.
**Success criteria:** expected resources are ready, the functional transaction passes, denied paths remain denied, metrics and logs are available, and cleanup/rollback is proven.
**Failure criteria:** an unexplained condition, missing evidence, hidden manual dependency, unsupported configuration, data loss, credential exposure, or inability to restore.

## 15. Troubleshooting methodology
Use an evidence-first chain. Do not jump directly to pod deletion, operator reinstall, SCC escalation, or credential replacement.
1. Clarify the exact user symptom, identity, namespace, resource, endpoint, payload, timestamp, and expected result.
2. Reproduce with the least-privileged identity and preserve the original error.
3. Check resource generation, conditions, events, finalizers, owner references, and observed generation.
4. Identify the controller responsible for the next state transition and inspect its recent logs.
5. Inspect generated pods, init containers, sidecars, probes, scheduling decisions, volumes, and security context.
6. Test service/endpoints/route/DNS/TLS/authentication as separate layers.
7. Test external dependencies such as object store, registry, database, Git, model source, and identity provider.
8. Correlate metrics, logs, audit events, node conditions, storage health, GPU health, and recent changes.
9. Apply the smallest reversible remediation and validate both positive and negative paths.
10. Create an RCA with trigger, contributing factors, detection gap, recovery actions, and prevention controls.
### 15.1 Known symptom families
#### Symptom: runtime not selected
- Confirm whether `runtime not selected` affects one user, one project, one node, one zone, one component, or the full cluster.
- Review events and status conditions before restarting anything.
- Identify the exact controller and dependency involved in `runtime not selected`.
- Compare a failing object with a known-good object from the same product version.
- Validate RBAC, NetworkPolicy, quota, storage, DNS, TLS, image access, and node placement.
- Capture logs with timestamps and correlation IDs; avoid unbounded log collection.
- Remediate through supported APIs or GitOps, then retest the user transaction.
- Add a preventive alert, policy, test, or capacity threshold for `runtime not selected`.
#### Symptom: unsupported model format
- Confirm whether `unsupported model format` affects one user, one project, one node, one zone, one component, or the full cluster.
- Review events and status conditions before restarting anything.
- Identify the exact controller and dependency involved in `unsupported model format`.
- Compare a failing object with a known-good object from the same product version.
- Validate RBAC, NetworkPolicy, quota, storage, DNS, TLS, image access, and node placement.
- Capture logs with timestamps and correlation IDs; avoid unbounded log collection.
- Remediate through supported APIs or GitOps, then retest the user transaction.
- Add a preventive alert, policy, test, or capacity threshold for `unsupported model format`.

## 16. Upgrade and change management
1. Read release notes, known issues, deprecations, support removals, and supported configurations.
2. Inventory CRD versions, custom runtimes/images, external databases, object stores, certificates, proxies, GPUs, and policy engines.
3. Back up declarative resources and stateful dependencies; verify restore, not merely backup completion.
4. Test upgrade in a representative environment and run automated pre/post functional transactions.
5. Freeze unrelated platform changes and define go/no-go criteria.
6. Monitor CSV, InstallPlan, operator logs, DataScienceCluster conditions, CRD conversion, and workload readiness.
7. Validate dashboard, project access, workbenches, pipelines, model registry, serving, TrustyAI, storage, GPU, and monitoring.
8. Review performance and cost because upgraded images or runtimes can change resource usage.
9. Document migration actions for deprecated resources and APIs.
10. Close the change only after an observation period and stakeholder validation.
## 17. HA and DR considerations
1. Classify each component as stateless, rebuildable state, durable state, or external dependency.
2. Do not assume increasing a Deployment replica count is supported; confirm operator-managed replica settings.
3. Use multi-zone node placement and storage only when the underlying infrastructure truly spans independent failure domains.
4. Protect databases and object stores with their native consistency and recovery mechanisms.
5. Back up Kubernetes resources, but understand that etcd/object YAML alone does not capture external artifacts or database state.
6. Store reproducible configuration in Git and immutable artifacts in governed registries/object stores.
7. Document RPO and RTO per service, not only for the cluster.
8. Test identity, certificates, DNS, routes, secrets, storage classes, and GPU capacity on the recovery cluster.
9. Define restoration order based on dependencies and controller reconciliation.
10. Run periodic game days and record measured recovery, data loss, manual steps, and gaps.
## 18. Corporate design-review questions
1. What exact user transaction defines success?
2. Which API object is the desired-state source of truth?
3. Which controller owns reconciliation and where are its logs?
4. Which state is stored in etcd, PVC, object storage, database, registry, or Git?
5. What breaks if the object store, database, identity provider, router, registry, or monitoring stack is unavailable?
6. Which features are GA, preview, deprecated, or unsupported in the deployed version?
7. How are identities, groups, service accounts, and external credentials separated?
8. How is tenant network, storage, secret, quota, and GPU isolation enforced?
9. What are the SLO, alert thresholds, and error-budget actions?
10. What is the capacity model and expansion lead time?
11. How is a new image, runtime, model, pipeline, or configuration promoted?
12. How are vulnerabilities, signatures, licenses, and provenance checked?
13. What is backed up, how often, where, encrypted by whom, and how is restore tested?
14. What is the upgrade path and migration plan for deprecated APIs?
15. What evidence is required to hand the service to L2 operations?
## 19. L3 scenario and incident workbook
Each drill is deliberately structured as a reusable incident, design-review, or change-exercise card.
### Drill 001 — development: runtime not selected during design
- **Situation:** a platform administrator reports `runtime not selected` while operating ServingRuntime in the development environment.
- **Primary object:** inspect `ServingRuntime` desired state, generation, status conditions, events, finalizers, and ownership.
- **Dependency hypothesis:** verify `KServe` before changing the OpenShift AI resource.
- **Evidence command:** `oc get events --sort-by=.lastTimestamp`.
- **Control review:** confirm that `RBAC` is working as designed and is not masking a capacity, identity, network, storage, or API problem.
- **Decision:** classify impact, blast radius, data risk, supportability, and rollback safety; provisional risk is **low** until evidence narrows it.
- **Remediation:** make the smallest supported, reversible change through GitOps or the owning API; never delete stateful resources without a verified backup and owner approval.
- **Validation:** repeat the end-to-end user transaction, check negative authorization/isolation tests, and observe metrics for a defined period.
- **Prevention:** add or improve an alert, policy, test, capacity threshold, runbook, architecture decision, or recovery exercise for `runtime not selected`.
### Drill 002 — development: runtime not selected during design
- **Situation:** a platform administrator reports `runtime not selected` while operating ServingRuntime in the development environment.
- **Primary object:** inspect `ClusterServingRuntime` desired state, generation, status conditions, events, finalizers, and ownership.
- **Dependency hypothesis:** verify `model server image` before changing the OpenShift AI resource.
- **Evidence command:** `oc get pods -A -o wide`.
- **Control review:** confirm that `NetworkPolicy` is working as designed and is not masking a capacity, identity, network, storage, or API problem.
- **Decision:** classify impact, blast radius, data risk, supportability, and rollback safety; provisional risk is **moderate** until evidence narrows it.
- **Remediation:** make the smallest supported, reversible change through GitOps or the owning API; never delete stateful resources without a verified backup and owner approval.
- **Validation:** repeat the end-to-end user transaction, check negative authorization/isolation tests, and observe metrics for a defined period.
- **Prevention:** add or improve an alert, policy, test, capacity threshold, runbook, architecture decision, or recovery exercise for `runtime not selected`.

- **Remediation:** make the smallest supported, reversible change through GitOps or the owning API; never delete stateful resources without a verified backup and owner approval.

### Drill 005 — development: runtime not selected during design
- **Situation:** a platform administrator reports `runtime not selected` while operating ServingRuntime in the development environment.
- **Primary object:** inspect `Container image` desired state, generation, status conditions, events, finalizers, and ownership.

## 21. Knowledge-check questions
1. How would you prove whether `ServingRuntime` or `KServe` is responsible for `runtime not selected` in ServingRuntime, and what evidence would you collect before remediation?
2. How would you prove whether `ClusterServingRuntime` or `model server image` is responsible for `unsupported model format` in ServingRuntime, and what evidence would you collect before remediation?

## 24. Closing guidance
A senior engineer should be able to build ServingRuntime, explain every dependency and reconciliation boundary, prove tenant and credential isolation, measure the service, recover it, and defend the design in an architecture or incident review.
The most reliable implementation is the one that is supportable, observable, reproducible, tested under failure, and owned by named teams—not the one with the largest number of enabled features.
